Over the last couple of years, as she took on coordinating the neighborhood garden, organizing oral history interviews, and helping with grandchildren’s schedules, the unstructured nature of her time made it harder for her to keep track of what she had promised to do and when. She found herself double-booking events, misplacing sign-up sheets, and forgetting routine self-care tasks like taking her vitamins or bringing needed materials to quilting meetings, which contrasted sharply with her past reputation for reliability and began to frustrate fellow volunteers and family members. Feeling embarrassed when a church outreach event she had agreed to coordinate nearly fell apart because of overlooked details, she mentioned her growing difficulty managing everyday responsibilities to her primary care doctor, who recommended a psychiatric evaluation to explore underlying attentional issues.\n- Chief Complaint: “Lately I keep forgetting everyday things I’m supposed to do, like taking my pills or bringing what I need to church events, even when I write them down.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): With the volunteer paperwork, I’ll think I’ve filled everything out properly, but later someone will point out that I skipped a whole section or wrote the wrong date because I just skimmed instead of really looking at it closely.\n- S002 (Sustained_Attention_Difficulty): When I sit down to read through the notes from our garden committee or follow a long Bible study lesson, my mind keeps drifting off after a few minutes and I realize I’ve missed half of what I just read or what people are saying.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start working on a plan for a church outreach or organizing the sign-up list for meals, and then I’ll wander off to put in a load of laundry or answer a call from one of the grandkids and never quite get back to finishing what I started.\n- S008 (Distractibility): I can be in the middle of making reminder calls for a church event and suddenly I’m staring out the window at a bird or thinking about what to cook for dinner, and before I know it I’ve forgotten who I already called.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Even with lists on the fridge and alarms on my phone, I still regularly forget simple things like taking my vitamins, bringing the right quilting supplies, or calling people back when I said I would, and I usually only remember hours later when it’s too late to be helpful.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): When I’m sitting through a long sermon or a meeting, I catch myself constantly tapping my foot, fiddling with my keys, or rearranging the papers in front of me because I just can’t seem to sit completely still.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit for a long time, like during a church council meeting, I start to feel this pent-up, jittery feeling inside, like I need to get up and move around or do something with my hands.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): I’ve tried things like quiet meditation at church or just sitting and listening to soft music, but after a few minutes I get antsy and start talking, humming along, or reaching for my phone or a crossword so I’m not just sitting there.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): Most days I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—checking on the garden, dropping off something for the great-grandkids, straightening the house—and I have a hard time just sitting down to rest without feeling like I should be doing the next task.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m waiting in line at the pharmacy or for my turn to speak at a meeting, I find myself checking the time over and over, shifting from foot to foot, and feeling irritated that it’s taking so long even though I know it’s not that bad.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I’ve noticed that when my kids or other volunteers are talking, I’ll jump in with my ideas or finish their sentences before they’re done, and sometimes I even start giving suggestions for their projects without being asked, then realize I’ve stepped on their toes.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but 'daydreamed too much,' forgot to turn in homework I’d actually done, and talked out of turn so much that they moved my seat more than once.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past couple of years, my mix-ups and forgotten commitments have made people at church and in my volunteer groups stop relying on me for important tasks, and my daughter has started double-checking my plans because she worries I’ll drop the ball with the grandkids’ schedules.\n- duration: These problems with keeping track of my everyday responsibilities and sticking with tasks have been steadily getting in the way for almost two years now, ever since my days became more Feeling embarrassed when a church outreach event she had agreed to coordinate nearly fell apart because of overlooked details, she mentioned her growing difficulty managing everyday responsibilities to her primary care doctor, who recommended a psychiatric evaluation to explore underlying attentional issues.\n- Chief Complaint: “Lately I keep forgetting everyday things I’m supposed to do, like closely.\n- S002 (Sustained_Attention_Difficulty): When I sit down to read through the notes from our garden committee or follow a long Bible study lesson, my mind keeps drifting off after a few minutes and I realize I’ve missed half of what I just read or what people are saying.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start working on a plan for a church outreach or organizing the sign-up list for meals, and then I’ll wander off to put in a load of laundry or answer a call from one of the grandkids and never quite get back to finishing what I started.\n- S008 (Distractibility): I can be in the middle of making reminder calls for a church event and suddenly I’m staring out the window at a bird or thinking about what to cook for dinner, and before I know it S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit for a long time, like during a church council meeting, I start to feel this pent-up, jittery feeling inside, like I need to get up and move around or do start talking, humming along, or reaching for my phone or a crossword so I’m not just sitting there.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): Most days I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—checking on the garden, dropping off something for the great-grandkids, straightening the house—and I have a hard time just sitting down to rest without feeling like I should be doing the next task.\n- S017 irritated that it’s taking so long even though I know it’s not that bad.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I’ve noticed that when my kids or other volunteers are talking, I’ll jump in with my ideas or Over the last couple of years, as she took on coordinating the neighborhood garden, organizing oral history interviews, and helping with grandchildren’s schedules, the unstructured nature of her time made it harder for her to keep track of what she had promised to do and when. She found herself double-booking events, misplacing sign-up sheets, and forgetting routine self-care tasks like taking her vitamins or bringing needed materials to quilting meetings, which contrasted sharply with her past reputation for reliability and began to frustrate fellow volunteers and family members. Feeling embarrassed when a church outreach event she had agreed to coordinate nearly fell apart because of overlooked details, she mentioned her growing difficulty managing everyday responsibilities to her primary care doctor, who recommended a psychiatric evaluation to explore underlying attentional issues.\n- Chief Complaint: “Lately I keep forgetting everyday things I’m supposed to do, like taking my pills or bringing what I need to church events, even when I write them down.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): With the volunteer paperwork, I’ll think I’ve filled everything out properly, but later someone will point out that I skipped a whole section or wrote the wrong date because I just skimmed instead of really looking at it closely.\n- S002 (Sustained_Attention_Difficulty): When I sit down to read through the notes from our garden committee or follow a long Bible study lesson, my mind keeps drifting off after a few minutes and I realize I’ve missed half of what I just read or what people are saying.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start working on a plan for a church outreach or organizing the sign-up list for meals, and then I’ll wander off to put in a load of laundry or answer a call from one of the grandkids and never quite get back to finishing what I started.\n- S008 (Distractibility): I can be in the middle of making reminder calls for a church event and suddenly I’m staring out the window at a bird or thinking about what to cook for dinner, and before I know it I’ve forgotten who I already called.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Even with lists on the fridge and alarms on my phone, I still regularly forget simple things like taking my vitamins, bringing the right quilting supplies, or calling people back when I said I would, and I usually only remember hours later when it’s too late to be helpful.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): When I’m sitting through a long sermon or a meeting, I catch myself constantly tapping my foot, fiddling with my keys, or rearranging the papers in front of me because I just can’t seem to sit completely still.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit for a long time, like during a church council meeting, I start to feel this pent-up, jittery feeling inside, like I need to get up and move around or do something with my hands.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): I’ve tried things like quiet meditation at church or just sitting and listening to soft music, but after a few minutes I get antsy and start talking, humming along, or reaching for my phone or a crossword so I’m not just sitting there.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): Most days I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—checking on the garden, dropping off something for the great-grandkids, straightening the house—and I have a hard time just sitting down to rest without feeling like I should be doing the next task.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m waiting in line at the pharmacy or for my turn to speak at a meeting, I find myself checking the time over and over, shifting from foot to foot, and feeling irritated that it’s taking so long even though I know it’s not that bad.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I’ve noticed that when my kids or other volunteers are talking, I’ll jump in with my ideas or finish their sentences before they’re done, and sometimes I even start giving suggestions for their projects without being asked, then realize I’ve stepped on their toes.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but 'daydreamed too much,' forgot to turn in homework I’d actually done, and talked out of turn so much that they moved my seat more than once.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past couple of years, my mix-ups and forgotten commitments have made people at church minutes and I realize I’ve missed half of what I just read or what people are saying.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start working on a plan for a church outreach or organizing S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit for a long time, like during a church council meeting, I start to feel this pent-up, jittery feeling inside, like I need to get up and move around or do something with my hands.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): I’ve tried things like quiet meditation at church or just sitting and listening to soft music, but after a few minutes I get antsy and start talking, humming along, or reaching for my phone or a crossword so I’m not just sitting there.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): Most days I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—checking on the garden, dropping off something for the great-grandkids, straightening the house—and I have a hard time just sitting down to rest without feeling like I should be doing the next task.\n- S017 Over the last couple of years, as she took on coordinating the neighborhood garden, organizing oral history interviews, and helping with grandchildren’s schedules, the unstructured nature of her time made it harder for her to keep track of what she had promised to do and when. She found herself double-booking events, misplacing sign-up sheets, and forgetting routine self-care tasks like taking her vitamins or bringing needed materials to quilting meetings, which contrasted sharply with her past reputation for reliability and began to frustrate fellow volunteers and family members. Feeling embarrassed when a church outreach event she had agreed to coordinate nearly fell apart because of overlooked details, she mentioned her growing difficulty managing everyday responsibilities to her primary care doctor, who recommended a psychiatric evaluation to explore underlying attentional issues.\n- Chief Complaint: “Lately I keep forgetting everyday things I’m supposed to do, like taking my pills or bringing what I need to church events, even when I write them down.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): With the volunteer paperwork, I’ll think I’ve filled everything out properly, but later someone will point out that I skipped a whole section or wrote the wrong date because I just skimmed instead of really looking at it closely.\n- S002 (Sustained_Attention_Difficulty): When I sit down to read through the notes from our garden committee or follow a long Bible study lesson, my mind keeps drifting off after a few minutes and I realize I’ve missed half of what I just read or what people are saying.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I’ll start working on a plan for a church outreach or organizing the sign-up list for meals, and then I’ll wander off to put in a load of laundry or answer a call from one of the grandkids and never quite get back to finishing what I started.\n- S008 (Distractibility): I can be in the middle of making reminder calls for a church event and suddenly I’m staring out the window at a bird or thinking about what to cook for dinner, and before I know it I’ve forgotten who I already called.\n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Even with lists on the fridge and alarms on my phone, I still regularly forget simple things like taking my vitamins, bringing the right quilting supplies, or calling people back when I said I would, and I usually only remember hours later when it’s too late to be helpful.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): When I’m sitting through a long sermon or a meeting, I catch myself constantly tapping my foot, fiddling with my keys, or rearranging the papers in front of me because I just can’t seem to sit completely still.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): If I have to sit for a long time, like during a church council meeting, I start to feel this pent-up, jittery feeling inside, like I need to get up and move around or do something with my hands.\n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): I’ve tried things like quiet meditation at church or just sitting and listening to soft music, but after a few minutes I get antsy and start talking, humming along, or reaching for my phone or a crossword so I’m not just sitting there.\n- S014 (Constant_Activity): Most days I’m bouncing from one thing to the next—checking on the garden, dropping off something for the great-grandkids, straightening the house—and I have a hard time just sitting down to rest without feeling like I should be doing the next task.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m waiting in line at the pharmacy or for my turn to speak at a meeting, I find myself checking the time over and over, shifting from foot to foot, and feeling irritated that it’s taking so long even though I know it’s not that bad.\n- S018 (Interrupting_Intruding): I’ve noticed that when my kids or other volunteers are talking, I’ll jump in with my ideas or finish their sentences before they’re done, and sometimes I even start giving suggestions for their projects without being asked, then realize I’ve stepped on their toes.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in grade school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but 'daydreamed too much,' forgot to turn in homework I’d actually done, and talked out of turn so much that they moved my seat more than once.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past couple of years, my mix-ups and forgotten commitments have made people at church and in my volunteer groups stop relying on me for important tasks, and my daughter has started double-checking my plans because she worries I’ll drop the ball with the grandkids’ schedules.\n- duration: These problems with keeping track of my everyday responsibilities and sticking with tasks have been steadily getting in the way for almost two years now, ever since my days became more unstructured after retirement and my husband passed.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Lately I keep forgetting everyday things I’m supposed to do, like taking my pills or bringing what I need to church events, even when I write them down.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Mention that it really started becoming a problem after your husband passed away and you retired, which left your days much more unstructured."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you into the clinic today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. Lately I keep forgetting everyday things I’m supposed to do, like taking my pills or bringing what I need to church events, even when I write them down. It’s been really embarrassing for me because I’ve always prided myself on being reliable, but I just can't seem to keep track of things anymore.\n\nDoctor: Along with these memory struggles, have you also been feeling persistently down, sad, or lost interest in the activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm feeling down or losing interest in my activities, as I actually still love being involved in everything. I'm just feeling really frustrated and embarrassed by these memory slips and how hard it's become to keep up with all the things I want to do.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to manage your daily activities, like handling your finances, cooking, or taking care of your home, completely on your own, or do you now need help from others?\n\nPatient: I still manage basic things on my own, like cooking and straightening up the house. However, I really struggle with daily tasks like remembering my pills, and my daughter has actually started double-checking my schedule because I keep mixing up dates.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you are still able to live and manage your daily life independently, even if it takes more effort, or do you now depend on others to get through the day safely?\n\nPatient: I can absolutely still live independently and manage on my own, though it takes a great deal of extra effort and I've had some setbacks. I don't need help to live safely, but my daughter has started double-checking my plans because she's worried I'll drop the ball with the grandkids' schedules. Sadly, people at church and my volunteer groups have also stopped relying on me for important tasks because of these mix-ups.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently been diagnosed with any medical conditions, or did you experience any physical illnesses, infections, or head injuries around the time these memory issues started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any new medical diagnoses or physical illnesses lately. I also haven't had any head injuries or infections around the time these issues started.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any long-standing chronic medical conditions, such as high blood pressure, diabetes, or thyroid issues, even if you've been treating them for a long time?\n\nPatient: To my knowledge, I don't have any major chronic conditions like high blood pressure, diabetes, or thyroid issues. I really just take some daily vitamins and supplements for my general health. Though, as I mentioned, those are some of the pills I keep forgetting to take!\n\nDoctor: Besides your memory, have you noticed other changes, like having trouble finding the right words when speaking, getting confused about the date or time, or getting lost in places you know well?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any trouble finding my words, and I never get confused about the date or get lost in familiar places. My struggles are strictly with keeping track of my daily tasks and staying focused on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you experienced a mini-stroke (TIA), a silent stroke, or any other brain-related condition in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been told anything like that. I've never had a mini-stroke, a silent stroke, or any other kind of brain-related condition.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory slips and difficulties keeping track of your schedule?"

I don't need help to live safely, but my daughter has started double-checking my plans because she's worried I'll drop the ball with the grandkids' schedules. Sadly, people at church and my volunteer groups have also stopped relying on me for important tasks because of these mix-ups.\n\nDoctor: Have you recently been diagnosed with any medical conditions, or did you experience any physical illnesses, infections, or head injuries around the time these memory issues started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any new medical diagnoses or physical illnesses lately. I also haven't had any head injuries or infections around the time these issues started.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any long-standing chronic medical conditions, such as high blood pressure, diabetes, or thyroid issues, even if you've been treating them for a long time?\n\nPatient: To my knowledge, I don't have any major chronic conditions like high blood pressure, diabetes, or thyroid issues. I really just take some daily vitamins and supplements for my general health. Though, as I mentioned, those are some of the pills I keep forgetting to take!\n\nDoctor: Besides your memory, have you noticed other changes, like having trouble finding the right words when speaking, getting confused about the date or time, or getting lost in places you know well?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any trouble finding my words, and I never get confused about the date or get lost in familiar places. My struggles are strictly with keeping track of my daily tasks and staying focused on what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you experienced a mini-stroke (TIA), a silent stroke, or any other brain-related condition in the past?\n\nPatient: No, I have never been told anything like that. I've never had a mini-stroke, a silent stroke, or any other kind of brain-related condition.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory slips and difficulties keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: These difficulties have been steadily getting in the way for almost two years now. It really started becoming a major problem after my husband passed away and I retired, which left my days feeling much more unstructured.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having trouble with your schedule and pills, but are you still able to handle complex tasks like managing your finances and paying bills entirely on your own?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any major issues with managing my finances or paying my bills. I still handle those tasks on my own, so I haven't thought about it much as being a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical tests, such as brain scans or blood work, specifically to investigate these memory issues since they started?"
  }

No, I haven't had any brain scans or specific blood work done for these issues. My primary care doctor simply referred me for this psychiatric evaluation after I brought up my difficulties to him.

I've never had a mini-stroke, a silent stroke, or any other kind of brain-related condition.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory slips and difficulties keeping track of your schedule?\n\nPatient: These difficulties have been steadily getting in the way for almost two years now. It really started becoming a major problem after my husband passed away and I retired, which left my days feeling much more unstructured.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having trouble with your schedule and pills, but are you still able to handle complex tasks like managing your finances and paying bills entirely on your own?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any major issues with managing my finances or paying my bills. I still handle those tasks on my own, so I haven't thought about it much as being a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any medical tests, such as brain scans or blood work, specifically to investigate these memory issues since they started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any brain scans or specific blood work done for these issues. My primary care doctor simply referred me for this psychiatric evaluation after I brought up my difficulties to him.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and scheduling difficulties been gradually getting worse over the past two years, or have they remained at about the same level of severity?"

My primary care doctor simply referred me for this psychiatric evaluation after I brought up my difficulties to him.\n\nDoctor: Have these memory and scheduling difficulties been gradually getting worse over the past two years, or have they remained at about the same level of severity?\n\nPatient: They have gradually become more of a constant, frustrating problem over the last two years. It started becoming much more noticeable after my husband passed and I retired, and as I took on more volunteer projects with unstructured time, keeping track of everything just got harder.\n\nDoctor: Have you or your family noticed any changes in your ability to make decisions, solve everyday problems, or use good judgment?"

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
